Answer: GAAP operating margin fell 1.7 percentage points to 19.1% from 20.8%, chiefly as raw-material/logistics inflation reduced margin 2.4 points, partly offset by 1.0 point from organic growth/productivity; special items were a further 1.7-point net drag (litigation, PFAS-manufacturing exit, Russia-exit, divestiture and restructuring costs, partly offset by the Food Safety divestiture gain). Operating margin remains useful, but adjusted margin better reflects underlying performance because these large special items materially distorted GAAP results. (3M 2022 10-K, pp. 20–21, 27)
Final: Answer: GAAP operating margin fell 1.7 percentage points to 19.1% from 20.8%, chiefly as raw-material/logistics inflation reduced margin 2.4 points, partly offset by 1.0 point from organic growth/productivity; special items were a further 1.7-point net drag (litigation, PFAS-manufacturing exit, Russia-exit, divestiture and restructuring costs, partly offset by the Food Safety divestiture gain). Operating margin remains useful, but adjusted margin better reflects underlying performance because these large special items materially distorted GAAP results. (3M 2022 10-K, pp. 20–21, 27)

Q: Does AMD have a reasonably healthy liquidity profile based on its quick ratio for FY22? If the quick ratio is not relevant to measure liquidity, please state that and explain why.

Q: What are the major products and services that AMD sells as of FY22?
⭐ GOLD (ground truth): AMD sells server microprocessors (CPUs) and graphics processing units (GPUs), data processing units (DPUs), Field Programmable Gate Arrays (FPGAs), and Adaptive System-on-Chip (SoC) products for data centers; CPUs, accelerated processing units (APUs) that integrate CPUs and GPUs, and chipsets for desktop and notebook personal computers; discrete GPUs, and semi-custom SoC products and development services; and embedded CPUs, GPUs, APUs, FPGAs, and Adaptive SoC products.

Answer: Operating margin fell 16.8 percentage points, from 22.2% in FY21 to 5.4% in FY22, chiefly from $3.5 billion of Xilinx/Pensando acquisition-related intangible amortization ($1.4 billion in cost of sales and $2.1 billion in operating expense), plus higher R&D from strategic investments and acquired/organic headcount and higher SG&A from headcount, go-to-market activity, and acquisition costs; this more than offset 44% revenue growth.
Final: Answer: Operating margin fell 16.8 percentage points, from 22.2% in FY21 to 5.4% in FY22, chiefly from $3.5 billion of Xilinx/Pensando acquisition-related intangible amortization ($1.4 billion in cost of sales and $2.1 billion in operating expense), plus higher R&D from strategic investments and acquired/organic headcount and higher SG&A from headcount, go-to-market activity, and acquisition costs; this more than offset 44% revenue growth.

{"document_id": "BOEING_2022_10K", "page_start": 50, "page_end": 50, "start": 0, "end": 4092, "text": "Table of Contents\nCritical Accounting Policies & Estimates\nAccounting for Long-term Contracts\nSubstantially all contracts at BDS and certain contracts at BGS are long-term contracts. Our long-term contracts typically represent a single\ndistinct performance obligation due to the highly interdependent and interrelated nature of the underlying goods and/or services and the\nsignificant service of integration that we provide.\nAccounting for long-term contracts involves a judgmental process of estimating the total sales, costs, and profit for each performance obligation.\nCost of sales is recognized as incurred, and revenue is determined by adding a proportionate amount of the estimated profit to the amount\nreported as cost of sales.\nDue to the size, duration and nature of many of our long-term contracts, the estimation of total sales and costs through completion is\ncomplicated and subject to many variables. Total sales estimates are based on negotiated contract prices and quantities, modified by our\nassumptions regarding contract options, change orders, incentive and award provisions associated with technical performance, and price\nadjustment clauses (such as inflation or index-based clauses). The majority of these long-term contracts are with the U.S. government where the\nprice is generally based on estimated cost to produce the product or service plus profit. Federal Acquisition Regulations provide guidance on the\ntypes of cost that will be reimbursed in establishing contract price. Total cost estimates are largely based on negotiated or estimated purchase\ncontract terms, historical performance trends, business base and other economic projections. Factors that influence these estimates include\ninflationary trends, technical and schedule risk, internal and subcontractor performance trends, business volume assumptions, asset utilization,\nanticipated labor agreements, and lingering impacts of COVID-19.\nRevenue and cost estimates for all significant long-term contract performance obligations are reviewed and reassessed quarterly. Changes in\nthese estimates could result in recognition of cumulative catch-up adjustments to the performance obligation’s inception to date revenues, cost\nof sales and profit in the period in which such changes are made. Changes in revenue and cost estimates could also result in a reach-forward\nloss or an adjustment to a reach-forward loss which would be recorded immediately in earnings. Net cumulative catch-up adjustments for\nchanges in estimated revenues and costs at completion across all long-term contracts, including the impact of increases in estimated losses on\nunexercised options, increased Loss from operations by $5,253 million, $880 million and $942 million in 2022, 2021 and 2020, respectively. The determination of the accounting quantity is limited by the ability to make reasonably\ndependable estimates.\nFactors that must be estimated include program accounting quantity, sales price, labor and employee benefit costs, material costs, procured part\ncosts, major component costs, overhead costs, program tooling and other non-recurring costs, and warranty costs. Estimation of the accounting\nquantity for each program takes into account several factors that are indicative of the demand for the particular program, such as firm orders,\nletters of intent from prospective customers and market studies. Total estimated program sales are determined by estimating the model mix and\nsales price for all unsold units within the accounting quantity, added together with the sales prices for all undelivered units under contract. The\nsales prices for all undelivered units within the accounting quantity include an escalation adjustment for inflation that is updated quarterly. Cost\nestimates are based largely on negotiated and anticipated contracts with suppliers, historical performance trends, and business base and other\neconomic projections. Factors that influence these estimates include production rates, internal and subcontractor performance trends, learning\ncurve, change incorporation, regulatory requirements in connection with certification, flight test and certification schedules, performance or\nreliability issues involving completed aircraft, customer and/or supplier claims or assertions, asset utilization, anticipated labor agreements,\ninflationary or deflationary trends, and lingering impacts of COVID-19.\nTo ensure reliability in our estimates, we employ a rigorous estimating process that is reviewed and updated on a quarterly basis. This includes\nreassessing the accounting quantity. Changes in estimates of program gross profit margins are normally recognized on a prospective basis;\nhowever, when estimated costs to complete a program plus costs already included in inventory exceed estimated revenues from the program, a\nloss is recorded in the current period.
